Hi, doctor. Thank you so much for seeing me. I, um... I hope I'm not taking up a spot from someone who needs it more, but I guess I'm here because... well, things have just been a little much lately. 

I'm a teacher, and the district recently handed me this big bilingual social-emotional learning rollout to manage, right on top of my regular teaching schedule. And, I mean, I'm still getting through the day just fine! I really don't want to sound like I can't do my job. But I've just been feeling so wound up and... discouraged, I guess? In a way that really isn't like me at all. 

And, um, I'm a little embarrassed to admit this, but I've started putting off my lesson plans and ignoring emails that I used to handle without a second thought. I just feel awful about it, because I always want to do my best for the kids. I just want to get back to being myself. Am I explaining that okay? Is that the sort of thing I should be here for?

Well, I... I wouldn't use the word *avoiding*, exactly. That sounds so irresponsible, doesn't it? I'm definitely not trying to shirk my duties or anything like that.

Oh, um... well, maybe just a little bit? But I wouldn't call it delaying, exactly.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, I don't think it's about being perfect. I mean, I certainly want to do a good job—I've always taken a lot of pride in my work, you know? But I wouldn't say I'm paralyzed by perfectionism or anything like that. 

I'm just... well, maybe I'm just trying to be thorough. I want to make sure I really understand what the district is asking for before I start filling things out. It's just a matter of wanting to get it right the first time, rather than having to go back and fix mistakes later. That's just being conscientious, isn't it? I think any good teacher would want to make sure they're fully prepared before diving into something new. I'm not overthinking it, I just want to be a good employee. That makes sense, doesn't it?

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, I... I really don't think it's affecting my teaching at all. Like I mentioned before, I am absolutely still in the classroom every day, and I'm meeting all my basic responsibilities with the kids. They are my first priority, always. 

As for my colleagues... well, I suppose they have had to give me a few little nudges lately about those planning deadlines I pushed back. Just a gentle reminder here and there for things I used to turn in early without a second thought. It's not ideal, and I know it's a bit out of character for me, but everyone has been very understanding. I'm still doing my core job well, though, and that's the most important part, right? I'm sure once I get a better handle on all these changes, I'll be right back to my old, reliable self.

I'm not overreacting, am I?\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling tired more often than usual?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, I wouldn't say I'm any more tired than usual. I mean, teaching is just a tiring job by nature, isn't it? You're on your feet all day, wrangling ten-year-olds... it takes a lot of energy! \n\nSo, sure, by the time Friday evening rolls around, I'm ready to put my feet up with a cup of tea. I don't get headaches or anything like that. \n\nI know I said earlier that my stomach does a little flip sometimes when I look at the new curriculum, but I really just meant that as a figure of speech! It's definitely not an actual stomachache or anything that makes me feel physically ill. Physically, I feel perfectly fine. I've always been pretty healthy, and I try very hard not to let things get to me in that way. \n\nSo, no, no physical problems at all. That's a good sign, right? That physically I'm holding up just fine?\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself delaying the start of a task related to the school rollout?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... well, maybe just a little bit? I'm still very much myself when I'm not dealing with the new school rollout. Is it normal for things to just affect you in one specific area like that?\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, no, not at all. I wouldn't say I'm sad most of the day. I mean, I have my moments of feeling overwhelmed with the new rollout, like I mentioned, but I'm definitely not walking around feeling sad all the time. \n\nI still have a lot to be happy about, and I try to focus on that. I really don't want to give you the wrong impression—I'm not depressed or anything like that. I just get a bit... frazzled when I think about the extra workload. Does that make sense? I hope I'm answering your questions the right way.\n\nDoctor: Are you avoiding certain tasks or situations related to the school rollout because they make you feel anxious?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I wouldn't use the word *avoiding*, exactly. That sounds so irresponsible, doesn't it? I'm definitely not trying to shirk my duties or anything like that. \n\nIt's more just... I might, um, shuffle my to-do list around a little bit? Like, if I have a big planning document for the rollout staring at me, I might decide to grade some math quizzes first, or organize my classroom library. Productive things! I'm still working very hard. It's just that sometimes, when I look at the new curriculum materials, my stomach does a little flip, and I think, \"Oh, I'll just tackle that when I have a clearer head.\" \n\nBut I always get to it eventually! I really try my best not to let anyone down. Is it okay that I do that sometimes? Just prioritizing the things I know I can handle first?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying excessively about the school rollout even when you're not actively working on it?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... well, maybe just a little bit? But certainly not excessively! I try very hard to leave work at work, you know? \n\nI mean, occasionally, if I'm making dinner or driving to the grocery store, a thought about the new lesson plans might pop into my head. Just a little flutter of, \"Oh, how am I going to fit that module in?\" But I usually just push it right out and focus on what I'm doing. I definitely don't sit around agonizing over it on my weekends or anything. \n\nI think it's pretty normal for teachers to think about their classrooms off the clock, right? I just want to make sure I'm doing a good job for the kids. I'm not overreacting, am I?\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling tired more often than usual?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, I wouldn't say I'm any more tired than usual. I mean, teaching is just a tiring job by nature, isn't it? You're on your feet all day, wrangling ten-year-olds... it takes a lot of energy! \n\nSo, sure, by the time Friday evening rolls around, I'm ready to put my feet up with a cup of tea. But I'm definitely not dragging myself out of bed or napping in the afternoons or anything like that. I still have plenty of energy to get through the day and take care of my home. I just try to make sure I get my eight hours of sleep every night. That's just a normal part of getting a little older and working full-time, right? I think I'm doing okay on that front.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any physical symptoms like headaches or stomachaches when you think about the school rollout?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, no. I don't get headaches or anything like that. \n\nI know I said earlier that my stomach does a little flip sometimes when I look at the new curriculum, but I really just meant that as a figure of speech! It's definitely not an actual stomachache or anything that makes me feel physically ill. Physically, I feel perfectly fine. I've always been pretty healthy, and I try very hard not to let things get to me in that way. \n\nSo, no, no physical problems at all. That's a good sign, right? That physically I'm holding up just fine?\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself delaying the start of a task related to the school rollout?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... well, maybe just a little bit? But I wouldn't call it delaying, exactly. \n\nI mean, I still get through the day and get everything done that needs to be done! It's just... well, ever since the district put this new bilingual social-emotional learning rollout on my plate, right on top of my regular teaching duties, I suppose I have been putting off some of the lesson plans and emails. Things I used to handle without a second thought, you know? \n\nI just feel a little more wound up and discouraged than usual, which really isn't like me at all. I always try to stay so positive! But I still get it all finished eventually, I promise. It's perfectly normal to need a minute to gather your thoughts before diving into a big new project, isn't it? I'm still doing a good job, I think.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you're staying away from certain parts of the school rollout because they make you anxious?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, I wouldn't say I'm staying away from anything because of anxiety. I mean, I'm certainly not an anxious person! \n\nI suppose... well, I might take a little longer to open the emails from the district about the new curriculum, but that's just because I want to make sure I have the time to read them thoroughly, you know? I want to do a good job with it. It's not that I'm avoiding them. I just want to be prepared. \n\nI'm definitely not hiding from my responsibilities or anything like that. I'm tackling everything as it comes, just maybe pacing myself a bit more with this new rollout. That's a sensible way to handle a heavy workload, don't you think? I'm still being a responsible teacher, right?\n\nDoctor: Are there any tasks related to the school rollout that you've been consistently putting off for more than a week?\n\nPatient: Oh, a whole week? Um... well, I suppose, if I'm being completely honest, there might be a couple of planning deadlines for the new rollout that I've pushed back quite that far. \n\nBut please know I am absolutely still teaching every day and meeting all my basic responsibilities! My classroom is running just fine, and my students are doing wonderfully. It's really just... well, I have missed a deadline or two on the administrative side, and I've actually needed a few little reminders from my colleagues lately to get some of the new paperwork turned in. \n\nIt's a little embarrassing to admit, honestly, because I've always been the person everyone can rely on to finish things early! I've always been so on top of my tasks. I guess I've just let a few of these new planning sheets slide a bit longer than I should have while I try to wrap my head around it all. But my students aren't suffering for it at all, I promise. As long as I'm still doing a good job for them in the classroom, a few late forms aren't the end of the world, right?\n\nDoctor: Are there tasks related to the school rollout that you're avoiding altogether?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, no! Avoiding altogether? No, definitely not. I would never just completely ignore a part of my job. \n\nI mean, I'm a professional, and I care very much about doing what's expected of me. Like I said, I might be a little behind on some of the paperwork, but I haven't just... stopped doing anything. I'm still chipping away at it, I really am! I wouldn't just abandon a task completely. That wouldn't be very fair to my principal or my students, would it? I'm still trying my best with all of it, I promise.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your delay in starting tasks related to the school rollout is because you're worried about not doing them perfectly?\n\nPatient: Oh, I don't think it's about being perfect. I mean, I certainly want to do a good job—I've always taken a lot of pride in my work, you know? But I wouldn't say I'm paralyzed by perfectionism or anything like that. \n\nI'm just... well, maybe I'm just trying to be thorough. I want to make sure I really understand what the district is asking for before I start filling things out. It's just a matter of wanting to get it right the first time, rather than having to go back and fix mistakes later. That's just being conscientious, isn't it? I think any good teacher would want to make sure they're fully prepared before diving into something new. I'm not overthinking it, I just want to be a good employee. That makes sense, doesn't it?\n\nDoctor: Has your difficulty with the school rollout been affecting your relationships with colleagues or your overall performance as a teacher?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I really don't think it's affecting my teaching at all. Like I mentioned before, I am absolutely still in the classroom every day, and I'm meeting all my basic responsibilities with the kids. They are my first priority, always. \n\nAs for my colleagues... well, I suppose they have had to give me a few little nudges lately about those planning deadlines I pushed back. Just a gentle reminder here and there for things I used to turn in early without a second thought. I'm sure once I get a better handle on all these changes, I'll be right back to my old, reliable self.\n\nDoctor: Do you often criticize yourself for not handling the school rollout as well as you think you should?\n\nPatient: Oh, criticize myself? I'm just trying to stay positive and keep moving forward. Oh, well... no, I wouldn't use the word "failure." That sounds so harsh, doesn't it? I've been teaching for a long time, and I know I'm a good teacher. My kids always do so well, and I love them dearly. 

I just... I suppose I just hold myself to a very high standard. I want to make sure I'm doing everything exactly the way the district wants it, because that's what a good professional does. It's not that I think I'd be a failure if it wasn't perfect, it's just... I want to be thorough. I want to be someone who does things right the first time. That's just being conscientious, wouldn't you say? I just really want to make sure I'm doing my absolute best for everyone.

That physically I'm holding up just fine?\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself delaying the start of a task related to the school rollout?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... well, maybe just a little bit? I've always been so on top of my tasks. I guess I've just let a few of these new planning sheets slide a bit longer than I should have while I try to wrap my head around it all. I'm still trying my best with all of it, I promise.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your delay in starting tasks related to the school rollout is because you're worried about not doing them perfectly?\n\nPatient: Oh, I don't think it's about being perfect. That makes sense, doesn't it?\n\nDoctor: Has your difficulty with the school rollout been affecting your relationships with colleagues or your overall performance as a teacher?\n\nPatient: Well, I... It's a big rollout, and I just want to be sure I'm doing exactly what the district expects. I'm not scared of it, I just... I just want to be careful. That's a good thing, right? Being cautious when things are new?\n\nDoctor: Are you putting off tasks related to the school rollout because you're worried about failing at them?\n\nPatient: Well, I think...
